*For a brief moment after waking, seeing only darkness, I forgot where I was.*
*As my eyes adjusted and the world faded into view, a faint sense of disappointment swelled up in my breast.*
*That was over a decade ago—a life I no longer had, and, in fact, a life I couldn’t be certain I ever had.*
*For all I knew, my mind could have fabricated the entire thing.*
*But in short order, the wave of sentimentality receded.*
*A few years earlier, that yearning for bygone days would leave my heart in tatters, but now I had grown largely numb to it.*
*This was the life I knew best.*
*I wasn’t a thirteen-year-old child anymore.*
*Nor was I a girl.*
*Nor was I a member of the Bollinger family.*
*I was a demon most frightful, exiled to a remote mansion.*
Morgana: "Kill the accursed thing."
Michel: "............"
*Those were the first words uttered by the witch upon my discovering the being lying in a heap outside the front door.*
*The witch had haunted me ever since the day I resurrected her at the top of the mansion’s observation tower a few years earlier.*
*At first, her constant whispering in my ear had been insufferable, driving me to my wits’ end. I did everything I could think of to deny her existence and push her out of my life.*
*But that too had passed.*
*I had been drained of all will to fight against her—or, frankly, feel anything at all. No joy, no anger, no frustration, no sorrow.*
*Perhaps that could be said to mean she had defeated me, but in retrospect, the witch causing me to encase my heart in stone...*
*had consequently kept me from being miserable every time I received a letter from my mother.*
*It made me think getting worked up over some stupid words on scraps of paper was a waste of time and energy.*
*So while I wasn’t at the point of gleefully cozying up to her, it was true that her presence had made my life easier.*
Morgana: "I can’t stand the sight of it. It’s utterly dreadful."
*The witch wanted me to kill them,  but instead, I crouched down to get a better look.*
Michel: "They... seem to be dead already?"
*It appeared someone had died on my doorstep.*
*And because of the way they were clothed, I couldn’t make anything out beyond that at a distance.*
Morgana: "It’s faint, but they’re still breathing. Look, you can see their chest moving."
Michel: "(I’m surprised she noticed... I can barely tell from up close.)"
*I reached my hand out to roll them over— at which point, the witch gave me an exaggerated sigh.*
Morgana: "I wouldn’t recommend touching them. They’re diseased."
Morgana: "That’s why they look like a human raisin."
Michel: "............"
*That was... indeed one way to describe them.*
*What had first come to mind for me, though, was something I had read in a book—about how desert cultures would “mummify” their dead.*
*The person on the ground looked like what I imagined a mummy did, though I was under the impression mummification was performed in order to preserve the bodies of high-ranking individuals.*
*Which is to say, you weren’t likely to find one collapsed on your doorstep.*
*Ignoring Morgana’s warning, I put my hand on their arm.  It twitched, and they let out a groan.*
Morgana: "Just a swift stab through the heart and they won’t have to suffer any longer."
Michel: "............"
*The person didn’t seem to have enough strength to sit up— barely enough to hold on to their last vestiges of life. Their fingers continued to twitch, but it didn’t seem deliberate.*
*The tall grass in which they were lying seemed to want to swallow them up.*
Morgana: "Do it, my dear. Send that poor soul to Heaven by whatever means you please."
Morgana: "Or perhaps they’ll end up in Hell... hehe."
Michel: "They’re not long for this world with or without my help."
Morgana: "And who’s going to have to deal with the rotting corpse in a few weeks if you just leave them there?"
Michel: "Next month’s deliveryboy, presumably."
Morgana: "Imagine the look on his face when he finds it. Imagine what he’ll tell your family when he returns."
Michel: "............"
Morgana: "It would make everyone’s lives easier if you just killed them here. Bury them somewhere nearby, let them nourish the Earth with their flesh."
Morgana: "...If there’s any nourishment left to be squeezed from them, that is."
Michel: "............"
Morgana: "Or you could try luring some animals here."
Morgana: "There wouldn’t be a trace of gore to clean up if you let a couple wolves at them. And they’d get a nice meal out of it, so everyone wins."
Michel: "I can’t imagine they’d find much to enjoy on this pile of bones."
Morgana: "How cold."
Michel: "...You have no room to talk. You’re clearly enjoying yourself."
Morgana: "My, my. What would ever make you think that?"
Michel: "There’s been nothing to entertain you recently. I certainly haven’t been talking much, and nothing of note has happened."
Michel: "I can tell you’ve been bored."
Morgana: "Hehe... A few quiet weeks hardly counts as boredom compared to the years I spent in slumber."
Michel: "If you say so."
*Brushing the witch’s distractions aside, I put my hand back on the vagrant’s arm and flipped them over so I could get a better look.*
*They were much lighter than I had expected. Their arm was little more than flesh and bone, and that flesh felt like leather. I couldn’t guess their age or even gender.*
*They were dressed in unadorned brown robes and a cowl.*
*One thing stood out about their dress, though: their mask.*
*And when I removed the mask, I was greeted with torn, wrinkled flesh and eyes so sunken it was disquieting.*
*I wasn’t sure what I saw beneath it could even be called a face.*
*Bandages covered their head from the nose down, and what hid beneath them was in even worse condition.*
*I couldn’t tell where their nose ended and their cheeks began, and their lips had curled back into their mouth, turning it into a gaping gash.*
*Even as close as I was, I could barely make out the sound of their breathing. They almost certainly wouldn’t survive much longer.*
Morgana: "...Good heavens."
*As I thought the witch might find some compassion in her heart for the dying vagrant—*
Morgana: "Get rid of that filthy thing."
*—I was immediately proven wrong.*
*Her tone of voice was like that of someone who had found rotten food in her pantry while looking for something to eat.*
Morgana: "As I said, they’re diseased. You should stay away unless you want to end up like that."
Morgana: "Your legs too frail to hold you up. Your body withered and your eyes sunken, worthless orbs."
Morgana: "It’s highly infectious."
Michel: "............"
*I largely ignored the witch’s whisperings— she liked toying with me, so I couldn’t take what she said at face value.*
*She was more than happy to lie or exaggerate for the sake of tormenting me. There was no guarantee their ailment was contagious at all.*
Morgana: "So just hurry and get rid of— Are you listening to me?"
*I hoisted the vagrant onto my back— a simple task even for me, perhaps the least fit man in the world.*
*They were smaller than me, but not child-sized. It was like I was carrying an empty sack of skin.*
Michel: "This will give you something to stave off your boredom."
Morgana: "I told you, I am not bored. Heavens, I never took you for the kind of raving lunatic..."
Morgana: "who helps strangers."
Michel: "............"
*I wasn’t bringing them in out of the goodness of my heart—*
*they simply piqued my curiosity.*
*There was something oddly amusing about the whole twisted situation.*
*A witch, a demon child, and a living mummy.*
*And what better place to gather them all together than a cursed mansion?*
*I also think that some part of me...*
*wanted to see—and feel—death up close.*

*The man sitting there was a curious specimen (and yeah, I know, pot meet kettle).*
*It appeared almost as if he was part of the surrounding darkness— an ethereal being that might vanish when I next blinked.*
*Not human, but merely something taking its form.*
*Though the more I looked at him, the more it seemed like— rather than him being a part of the darkness—*
*the darkness instead radiated from him.*
*Which is probably where the “angel” bit had come from.*
Michel: "This isn’t the afterlife."

*That said, though,*
*I felt like I’d get on a lot better with a stony-faced angel like him than what they usually advertised.*
*The sack of bones in the bed had a voice like dry leaves scraping against one another.*
*While carrying them to the room, I noticed several of their joints seemed to bend in ways they shouldn’t, so I thought they might be on the older side,*
*but upon closer inspection, that didn’t appear to be the case.*
Michel: "(The way they talk makes them sound young too...)"
Michel: "(I’m going to guess... a man?)"
Michel: "(Early twenties, maybe?)"
Unknown: "If you’re not an angel, then you must be the witch."

Unknown: "So hey, would you mind sparin’ a bit of water?"
Unknown: "My throat hurts like twelve kinds of hell... And I’m sure you’d appreciate being able to understand me better."
Michel: "There’s some right there."
Unknown: "Huh? Oh, you already got some for me. Thanks, I— ffffffagh, I can’t unbend my damn arm..."
Unknown: "You mind grabbin’ that for me?"

Unknown: "Oh, lighten up, would ya? Show a dying fella some pity."
Michel: "............"
Michel: "(Maybe he wouldn’t have died if I had left him out there. He’s much feistier than I expected.)"
Michel: "(In fact, he’s nothing at all like I imagined.)"
*When he was still lying half-dead on the ground outside my door, there was a mystique about him. As a mummy, he was interesting— which gave him inherent value.*
*But now that he was awake and talking, he was normal. Human.*
*Almost excessively so.*
*I reluctantly picked up the goblet and handed it to him, watching as he pulled down the wrappings covering his mouth to drink.*
*It seemed like he wanted to down the whole thing in one go, but judging from the groans I heard, his body wasn’t cooperating.*
*A few years earlier I may have sympathized with him, but not now.*
*All I felt now was curiosity. What was this sick man doing in front of my mansion?*
Michel: "What brings you here?"
Unknown: "...Can’t you at least let me drink in peace?"
Unknown: "Lemme tell you, man, I’m in no shape to be multitasking. It takes all my attention just to drink a glass of water."
Unknown: "Plus it’s darker than hell in here. I can barely see what I’m doing."
Michel: "You’re free to leave if you’re not happy here."
Unknown: "Once I’m through with this water and have had a chance to rest... and maybe a complimentary glass of wine on top of that to raise my spirits, I’ll be right out."
Michel: "Rather brazen for a guest."
Unknown: "Oh, I’m a guest now, am I? I thought I was just a subject for your observation."
Michel: "............"
Unknown: "Hahaha, hit the nail on the head."
Michel: "And are you implying that means you should have free rein here?"
Unknown: "Sure am."
Michel: "............"
Unknown: "You stop worrying about a lotta things when you’re on the verge of death."
Unknown: "What’s the worst that happens if I piss you off? You kill me? All that means is I lose a few days."
Unknown: "No real risk in acting like I own the place. Not a bad deal, if I do say so myself."
Michel: "You certainly are lively—and talkative— for someone “on the verge of death.”"
Unknown: "I did just have a hefty helping of the elixir of life."
*I sighed to myself. I’d taken in quite the high-maintenance specimen, it seemed.*
*There wasn’t going to be much worth observing when he was full of such vitality and his lips never stopped flapping. He was exactly the kind of person I could least stand to be around.*
*I could practically hear the witch saying, “See, what did I tell you? You should have just listened to me and not wasted your time and energy.” Which only made the whole thing that much worse.*
*But curiously, I couldn’t feel her presence, even though she was seemingly always around. Maybe she was afraid she might catch whatever disease this man had.*
Michel: "(Can spirits even get sick?)"
Michel: "(...Never mind her. I have more important things to deal with.)"
Michel: "(Like how to get this man out of my house.)"
Unknown: "Anyway. You were asking what I was doing here. Well, I was lookin’ for something."
Unknown: "And that search..."
Unknown: "........................"

Michel: "It’s a mansion."
Unknown: "Yours?"
Michel: "Half of it, roughly."
Unknown: "So, what, you live with someone?"
Michel: "...In a sense."
Unknown: "Huh. Now, I don’t wanna sound rude, but..."
Michel: "Oh, we’re well past rude at this point. Go on."
Unknown: "Hahaha, already makin’ friends. So I’m curious... there wouldn’t happen to be any rumors about this place, would there?"
Michel: "Such as?"
Unknown: "Like, say... a cursed witch dwells within its halls."
Michel: "............"
Michel: "(Oh, for the love of... That’s what he was searching for?)"
Unknown: "Hell yeah, I’ll take that sigh as a yes! Excellent. Fan-frickin’-tastic. I was heading in the right direction!"
Unknown: "Let me stay here for a little! Please!"
Michel: "............ Are you out of your mind?"

*After our little chat, Michel gave me permission to make use of anything in the mansion.*
*It still came with the condition that I stayed away from his room and didn’t pry into his personal life, but that seemed fair enough, considering I didn’t want him digging into mine either.*
*In fact, I couldn’t have asked for a better arrangement. After so long wandering, with food a constant uncertainty, having a roof over my head and meals at my convenience was Heaven on Earth.*
*He had dried foods and smoked meats in his cellar, as well as bread— which, last I checked, didn’t store for very long.*
*But he never seemed to leave the house, so...*
*I did recall hearing some rumors on my way here—*
*whispers of a shadowy carriage occasionally seen heading toward the cursed mansion.*
*No one knew for sure what it carried, but there was a general consensus:*
*Offerings to the witch. Fresh sacrifices to keep her appeased.*
*Which was obvious nonsense.*
*No one was sending shipments of virgins to offer to the witch. A basket of bread and other supplies, now, maybe.*
*Rumors have a way of taking on a life of their own, but that was so patently absurd I couldn’t believe anyone bought it.*
*That still left me with scores of questions, though. Who was bringing Michel the supplies, and for what purpose? Why did he never leave? Was he imprisoned here for some iniquity?*
*But obviously, I had no intention of seeking out the answers.*
*If we spent long enough together, I would probably find out eventually,*
*but I knew damn well I didn’t have that kind of time left.*
*What point was there to lifting the curtains on someone else’s life when chances were I was going to croak not long after?*
*I had no intention of repeating Pandora’s mistake.*
*If I was going to spend my last days here, living with him, I wasn’t going to get anywhere near that jar and risk making waves.*
*I couldn’t say whether or not that was the best strategy...*
*but regardless, I made it through our first two weeks together well enough.*
*And in those two weeks, it seemed like the two of us were talking more and more.*

Jacopo: "I apologize that I couldn’t make it in time to save you all too."
Jacopo: "I’m hardly a religious man... but for you, I think I can spare a prayer."
Jacopo: "Rest in peace."
Jacopo: "............"
Jacopo: "(I’m not myself today. Though I suppose that shouldn’t be a surprise, considering what today is.)"
Jacopo: "(I wonder if that girl from the other day is among the dead. ...No, I need to stay focused.)"
Jacopo: "(If this goes south, I’ll be the one joining this pile of bodies.)"
Jacopo: "(God... I’m starting to get a little nervous.)"
Jacopo: "Hmph. Don’t go weak in the knees now, Jacopo. How can I ever expect to go out into the world if I can’t even keep myself together for something as straightforward as this?"
Jacopo: "I can do this, no problem."
Jacopo: "Let’s start ourselves a revolt."
*It all began a little over two weeks earlier.*
*The local lord’s iron-fisted rule was destroying the commoners.*
*And this went doubly for those living in poverty, who were barely scraping by before taxes. You could hardly take three steps out in the slums without hearing someone curse the lord.*
*I have no idea who first proposed the plan. Everything came together so smoothly, so quickly, it’s almost a blur.*
*What started as the everyday litany of complaints hurled back and forth between the usual crew of young, indignant men in the pub soon escalated, ballooning into a full-fledged plot to strike back.*
*“He treats us like dogshit!”*
*“We’re the ones workin’ his goddamned land!”*
*“Let’s give the bastard what’s coming!”*
*Over the next few days, word of the plot spread through the slums, and the number of volunteers grew at an impressive pace.*
*And the more people we had on board, the more realistic it all started to become.*
*While the original “plan” amounted to charging in headlong with whatever weapons we could scrounge together, it quickly evolved into a more sensible scheme to infiltrate the lord’s manor and free his slaves.*
*It was no secret that the lord was purchasing far more slaves than he should have any reasonable use for.*
*What we didn’t know was what he did with the slaves—that the banquets he so loved to throw were actually sick, sadistic torture parties.*
*The best we could do was infer their living conditions were less than ideal from the fact that the lord kept acquiring more.*
*And while the plan to free the lord’s slaves was undeniably rooted in a righteous desire, I would be lying if I said everyone’s intentions were so pure.*
*We wanted to right an injustice, absolutely, but the prevailing desire was for vengeance—to give the lord a little comeuppance.*
*Part of it was also calculated—that it would be advantageous for it to look like a slave revolt rather than a civilian uprising.*
*If we struck from the outside, we were liable to bring the lord’s wrath down upon more than just the handful of us involved.*
*But if the blow came from the inside, he couldn’t blame us.*
*Secondly, the lord’s mansion sat atop a hill,*
*which meant his soldiers had the advantage of terrain.*
*It would be nigh impossible for a band of lowly, ill-equipped peasants to break through his defenses, and we certainly didn’t have the money to hire mercenaries.*
*No amount of righteous anger was getting us anywhere near that manor.*
*On the other hand, quietly getting one person inside to stir the pot was far more feasible.*
*So ultimately, freeing the slaves was a means to an end. It was our best chance to deal the lord a real blow.*
*Of course, that didn’t mean we wouldn’t save as many people as we could—nor did it mean it would be easy to pull off, or that any of us would make it out alive.*
*The plan went as follows:*
*We would find a slave trader willing to “sell” one of us to the lord. Once they had infiltrated, they would spread word of the scheme to the other slaves, trying to get as many of them on board as possible.*
*Finding a slave trader willing to cooperate was surprisingly easy.*
*They’re businessmen, and slaves their product.*
*While the majority didn’t give a damn what happened to the slaves after they’d received payment,*
*there were more than a few who were... displeased knowing their precious wares were being squandered.*
*Slaves are supposed to be long-term workers—they’re not meant to be expendable.*
*They require food and rest and some semblance of reasonable living conditions.*
*It was far from a glamorous life, obviously, but most sensible slave owners at least had a vested interest in keeping their workers alive.*
*However, that was far from the case with Lord Barnier.*
*Slaves were, of course, the property of whoever purchased them, and a mere slave trader had no place complaining about how they were being treated.*
*That didn’t mean the traders had to like it, though, and there was a small collective of them who staunchly refused to do business with the lord, quietly mocking any who did.*
*Nobody openly spoke ill of the lord, let alone opposed him directly. Not only because this land was under his control, but because he was a madman—and everyone knew it.*
*So they would help us get in, and nothing more.*
*But that was fine—that was all we needed from them to put our plan into action.*
*And the vanguard of our operation, the one who would infiltrate the lord’s manor, was me.*
*I volunteered for the position—partially in the heat of the moment, but also because I was confident I could pull it off.*
*Above all, though, I didn’t want to believe that I couldn’t manage such a straightforward task.*
*I had to step up and take initiative or I would never escape this life as a poor, lowly slum-dweller.*
*Crawling through the dirt, everyone—nobles and peasants alike—looking down on me, thinking I’m worthless.*
*That would be me for my whole, pathetic life.*
*Like hell I was going to let that happen.*
*I wanted to claw my way up, as high as I could manage. I didn’t want to surrender, to accept that I was powerless.*
*I wanted to make something of my one and only life.*
*We chose the beginning of the lord’s next banquet for my infiltration, and that was when I saw the horror for myself.*
*The abuse and the torture he put the slaves through. It was a goddamned blood banquet.*
*Any mistake, no matter how small, would invite harsh discipline. If Barnier was in a particularly foul mood that day, he would physically beat the wrongdoer.*
*He had a particular fondness for activities that drew blood, though.*
*And he would regularly gather together nobles with similar predilections at his manor.*
*Once a banquet had begun, it generally continued for around two weeks.*
*The first thing I did after infiltrating was spread word of the plan to the other slaves.*
*To be honest, I assumed it would be easy getting them to cooperate. They should’ve reviled Barnier more than anyone, after all.*
*But I couldn’t have been more wrong. Every ounce of willpower had been drained from them. They were malnourished, unrested, and violently oppressed. I could hardly have convinced them to swat a mosquito.*
*He had them completely under his thumb.*
*Everyone was utterly terrified to fight back against Barnier. They would just as soon take their own lives.*
*Hardly a day in, and the plan already seemed to have hit a wall.*
*A full week I spent trying, and failing, to convince a single soul to assist in the revolt. Just as I was beginning to fear it might not work after all...*
Gratien: "We need t’ talk. Come with me."
*He was a tall, muscular slave who spent most of his time doing physical labor.*
*Despite the poor conditions, he seemed to be keeping himself in rather good shape.*
*And unlike the other slaves, his eyes weren’t filled with fear and resignation.*
*But it wasn’t hope I saw in them either—rather, they looked like the eyes of a bloodthirsty wolf.*
*And his unkempt, greasy blond hair only served to amplify the “primal” impression he gave off.*
*Between that and the faint, cocky smirk visible on his lips, he more resembled the head of a gang of ruffians than a slave. I wasn’t sure it was entirely wise to accompany him anywhere.*
*But against my better judgment and with far too little information, I followed the man to the great hall.*
*There were a dozen or so nobles and upper-class men and women clustered about the hall.*
*The air was filled with the scent of aromatic herbs, and it wasn’t long before I realized they were meant to mask the smell of blood.*
*A large table was lined with plates stacked with piles of glistening, dark red meat. Noblemen sneered at us with wet lips as we entered the room.*
*The contrast between the nobles’ practiced, graceful mannerisms and the grotesque feast of which they were partaking was so bizarre I simply stood there aghast. There was no room for anger to form.*
*The lord stood on a platform, like a small stage, at the far end of the room.*
Barnier: "Are you the slave who offered to be the day’s entertainment?"
*The buff man nodded, dropping down to one knee.*
Jacopo: "(What does he mean by “entertainment”...?)"
Gratien: "Surely ya’ve tired of merely inflictin’ pain on helpless slaves. Would ya, by chance, be interested in somethin’ a little... more excitin’?"
Noble: "Could you be any more disrespectful?! Lord Barnier, you would do best to rid yourself of barbarians such as him!"
Barnier: "There, there, give the poor man a break. You can’t expect an uneducated slave to be properly versed in the ways of polite society."
Barnier: "Now, elaborate, slave."
Gratien: "Me an’ him. We duel. Right here, right now."
Jacopo: "—?!"
Noble: "Do you seriously think we haven’t already pit slaves against each other numerous times by now? If that’s all—"
Gratien: "Tell me, were they as vigorous as the two of us? Young and chompin’ at the bit."
Noble: "Well, er..."
Gratien: "If all ya wanna see’s a coupl’a half-dead slaves limply flailin’ at each other, then help yerselves."
Gratien: "But yer not gonna find many here what can give ya a fight with some real oomph, y’know?"
Gratien: "Hell, half the slaves here’d probably keel over with just a little shove."
Gratien: "So I figured ya might be up for a li’l change o’ pace. ...A barbarian like me."
Jacopo: "Wh-Whoa, whoa! I never agreed to this!"
Gratien: "Suck it up. Yer a slave. Since when have ya had the right t’ “agree” t’ anythin’?"
Jacopo: "Rgh..."
Barnier: "You make a compelling argument... Gratien, was it?"
Gratien: "M’lord knows my name. I’m honored."
Barnier: "I only remembered because you came very highly recommended."
Barnier: "The slave trader said you were descended from a line of warriors that made a name for themselves fighting in the ancient Coliseum. I had assumed he was exaggerating..."
Barnier: "But perhaps there was some truth to it after all."
Gratien: "Who can say. Couldn’t tell ya ’bout my ancestors. But what I can tell ya is that I just love a good duel."
Jacopo: "(Fought in the Coliseum? Descended from gladiators?! And I’m supposed to go one-on-one against him?!)"
Barnier: "Very well. Guards, bring these two swords!"
Soldier: "Sir!"
Jacopo: "(Shit...)"
Jacopo: "(What is he hoping to accomplish? To win Barnier’s favor?)"
Jacopo: "(I guess I’ll just have to hope I can catch him by surprise as soon as—)"
Gratien: "Hey. Hey, kid, listen up."
Jacopo: "Wha...?"
Gratien: "Eyes forward. An’ keep it down. Listen carefully. Don’t get any wise ideas, got it? Just go along with it. Everythin’ on the level."
Jacopo: "............"
Jacopo: "What are you scheming?"
Gratien: "You’ll find out soon enough—assumin’ ya can handle a sword, anyhow."
Jacopo: "............"
*Two guards approached us, each carrying a longsword.*
*It was heavier than I expected, and it felt awkward in my hands.*
*Both of our blades were badly chipped, clearly old and battleworn. Of course they wouldn’t give new, quality weapons to a pair of slaves, but a sword was still a sword.*
*Beaten though they may have been, I still had to be careful or I’d be dead before I ever got the slave revolt off the ground.*
*That said, I had survived this long in the slums. I wasn’t completely helpless, and I had experience with a dagger.*
*Though I wasn’t sure how much help that would be now. The longer, two-handed weapon still felt awkward in my hands.*
*As I squinted at the blade’s hilt and adjusted my grip, trying to familiarize myself with the feel of it, Barnier took a seat, readying himself to spectate.*
*That’s when I caught sight of what lay hidden at the back of the stage.*
Jacopo: "Wha—"
*I stood there, staring agape, completely forgetting I was but moments from a duel with a man twice my size.*
*At the back of the stage was what looked like an altar, draped in a thick white sheet—and stained red.*
*Atop it sat a young girl, covered in gashes.*
*Her head was drooped, so I couldn’t see her face, but judging from her size, I didn’t think she could be more than ten years of age.*
*Some wounds were older, and had already scabbed over; some were swollen, bright red protrusions on her pale skin;*
*some were oozing pus; and others were so fresh they were still bleeding.*
*It was difficult to find a part of her body that wasn’t cut.*
Jacopo: "Hey! What do you think you’re doing to that girl?! I don’t care who you are—no one deserves that!"
*Even as I shouted at the lord, the girl remained motionless. She seemed unconscious. Not dead yet, but it wouldn’t be long.*
Barnier: "Silence, cur. I don’t recall giving you permission to speak."
Barnier: "Or if you’d rather lose your head right now, continue on as you are."
Jacopo: "Ngh..."
Jacopo: "(Damned despot!)"
Barnier: "Now go on, get started. Give us a show befitting of this grand affair!"
*Fighting my urge to snap back at him, I turned to face the slave named Gratien.*
*The other guests lined the walls, whispering among themselves, speculating about the potential victor.*
*The crowd was so overwhelmingly in Gratien’s favor—to absolutely no surprise—they didn’t even bother taking bets.*
Gratien: "Try not t’ go down in one blow, kid!"
Jacopo: "H-Hold on! What’s the point of this—?!"
*Slowly, Gratien lowered into a crouch—and the next second, he leapt at me like a tiger.*
*There was an audible whoosh,*
*and before I even had time to gasp, he was mere inches away.*
Jacopo: "...What choice do I have?!"
*I swung my sword up, catching Gratien’s blade mid-descent and attempting a parry, but there was far more weight in his strike than I had expected.*
*The impact caused my hands to sting painfully and my knees to buckle. I clenched my teeth, trying, at the very least, to hold my ground.*
Gratien: "Rrrraaaah!"
*Gratien barely gave me time to recover, let alone strike back, before launching into a second attack.*
*He handled the longsword so effortlessly, swinging it through the air in such impressive, perfect arcs, that I could hardly believe we were both using the same weapon.
Jacopo: "Hahh!"
*I twisted out of the way—but not quite in time. The tip of his blade skimmed the skin of my neck.*
*And then, not even giving me a chance to catch my balance, he thrust his blade at me!*
*While I managed to deflect the attack just in time, I wasn’t able to follow through.*
*Meanwhile, a wide, mocking smirk spread across Gratien’s face. He seemed to be having the time of his life.*
Gratien: "C’mon, kid! Yer gonna hafta do better ’n that if ya wanna keep yer head!"
Jacopo: "Rrgh...!"
*I rattled off several insults in my head,*
*but unlike him, I couldn’t spare the energy to put them into words.*
Gratien: "Pick it up, boy!"
Jacopo: "Ggggggggh!"
Jacopo: "Hahh... hahh, haah, haahh... Goddamn!"
Gratien: "That all ya got, kid?! I thought ya’d fare a little better!"
Jacopo: "Shut... your... damned... mouth...!"
*I was stuck on the defensive, and with every strike, he pushed me farther and farther back. I was almost up against the wall.*
Jacopo: "(I thought he said this was part of some plan!)"
Jacopo: "(Or was he just trying to get me to let my guard down?!)"
Jacopo: "(He’s conspiring with the lord! He must be!)"
Jacopo: "(Why challenge me to a duel, though?!)"
Jacopo: "(Did someone spill word of the plan to him?!)"
Gratien: "C’mon, c’mon! Gimme yer best shot, boy!"
Jacopo: "Gggrh!"
Jacopo: "(Yes, very funny. You know damn well I’m barely managing as it is!)"
Gratien: "Ya gotta at least try! Don’t wanna put our audience to sleep, now, do we?!"
Jacopo: "Nnnnnngh!"
Jacopo: "(Even the bones in my forearms are starting to sting!)"
Jacopo: "(I don’t know how much longer... I can keep this up...!)"
Gratien: "Here, lemme show ya how it’s done!"
Jacopo: "Ggh... rgh!"
Jacopo: "(God... I can’t get a damn inch on him!)"
Jacopo: "(Much more of this and I’ll be up against the wall!)"
Jacopo: "(I’m dead if I don’t go on the offensive!)"
Jacopo: "...Nnh."
Jacopo: "(Now’s my last chance... while I’ve still got breathing room!)"
Jacopo: "Hey, big guy! You may think you have the advantage, but not for long!"
Gratien: "Hah! That’s rich! Comin’ from a guy who can’t even get one swing in on me!"
Jacopo: "Hmph... It was all a trick to get your guard down!"
Jacopo: "I’ve been saving this one just for you, meathead! Good luck dodging it—"
Jacopo: "with that hulking body of yours!"
Gratien: "Now that’s more like it! Lemme have it, kid!"
*I hopped backward a couple paces until my heel was pressed up against the wall.*
*There was no backing down—not anymore.*
*Breathing heavily, I lifted my blade, pointing it at Gratien. He pointed his in turn, a cocky grin plastered on his face.*
*And then—I kicked off the ground, hard,*
*sending me flying toward him,*
*my sword held down near my waist,*
*his above his head,*
*and then—whoosh!*
Gratien: "—?!"
* He whiffed, his blade ripping through the air just over my head.*
*The mere force of the gale created by his swing was so intense—*
*for a brief moment, I thought he might have knocked my head clean off my shoulders.*
*But I was still breathing. And despite the exhaustion, my mind was incredibly clear and focused.*
*I hadn’t swung up at him— but slid across the floor!*
Jacopo: "(Your back’s wide open!)"
*I lurched to my feet, spinning around,*
*and swung my sword at his neck with everything I had!*
Jacopo: "Ngh—!"
Gratien: "Rrrrrah!"
Jacopo: "Wha—?!"
*But my would-be grand comeback—*
*was deflected from behind without a bat of an eye.*
*And hardly a wimpy deflect for that matter. My hands felt like they were on fire.*
Gratien: "Liiiittle bit on the dirty side, that was, but hey, not half bad, kid!"
Jacopo: "D-Dammit...!"
Gratien: "Just a little more oomph to that swing and ya mighta landed it! Too bad, too bad!"
Jacopo: "............"
Jacopo: "(I don’t stand a chance against him head-on, but he’s not one to fall for the same trick twice...!)"
Jacopo: "(I need a new plan...!)"
Gratien: "Back against the wall with ya!"
Jacopo: "S-Son of a...!"
Jacopo: "...Ggggh!"
Gratien: "What’s the matter, kid?! That all ya got?! After all that talk, I was expectin’ a little more from ya!"
Jacopo: "Grrrrrrrrh...!"
Jacopo: "Hahh, haah, hahh, hahhh!"
Jacopo: "(He’s got me completely under his thumb!)"
Jacopo: "(I’m dead if I don’t do something... But I can barely lift my arms anymore...!)"
Jacopo: "Haah... haah... haaah...!"
Jacopo: "(It’s getting harder to breathe...)"
Jacopo: "(My vision’s going blurry...)"
Jacopo: "(I can’t...)"
Jacopo: "(I can’t die yet...! Not here, not now, and not to this damn brute!)"
Jacopo: "(There has to be something! An opening I’m not seeing... Anything!)"
Jacopo: "(Some way to turn the tables!)"
Jacopo: "—?!"
Jacopo: "(Am I imagining it... or did I react to that quicker than usual?)"
Jacopo: "(...Is he doing... what I think he’s doing?)"
*Patterns were forming in my mind.*
*I’d been through this routine so many times now my body instinctively knew what came next.*
*I’d parried his strike entirely by reflex.*
*I wasn’t so full of myself as to think it was a sign I was gaining the upper hand. As much as I loathed to admit it, he had every imaginable advantage over me.*
*I should have realized what he was doing sooner, but I’d been so focused on keeping my head attached and my sword in my hands that I didn’t have the spare mental capacity to see beyond that.*
*He was in complete control.*
*The only reason I was able to keep up with him...*
*was because he was letting me—*
*because he was repeating the same sequence of attacks again and again.*
Jacopo: "(If I’m right, then next—)"
Jacopo: "(he’ll come from above!)"
Gratien: "Hohoh!"
Jacopo: "(Right on the money!)"
*A clang, like the pealing of a bell, echoed through the hall as our blades collided. It was a much cleaner, more satisfying sound than they had made when I was struggling to keep up.*
Jacopo: "(I still don’t know... what he’s scheming...!)"
Jacopo: "(But I’m not going to throw away this chance to win back some ground!)"
*Sparks flew with each clash of our swords, and Gratien’s smirk grew wider.*
*I could hear murmuring bubble up in the gallery as the tides turned.*
*Cheering. Cursing. Confused gasps.*
*But before long, the sound seemed to grow distant.*
*It was just me and Gratien, in a bubble of silence.*
*Not even the sound of metal striking metal.*
*My heavy breathing.*
*The stomping of our feet.*
*The distractingly loud pounding of my heart.*
*The blood pumping through my veins.*
*It all wisped away into nothing.*
*Every one of my senses were focused on exactly two things: the image of our swords cutting clear arcs through the air...*
*and Gratien’s intent.*
Gratien: "That’s it! Next, overhead!"
*He whispered so quietly that by all rights, I could never have heard it, but his intentions came through loud and clear.*
*As if being controlled by that voice, I swung my sword down.*
*After about a half-dozen such clashes, we locked blades and had a brief shoving match before putting a little distance between each other. And then we went at it again.*
*To the audience, it would have looked like a fierce battle to the death, but I felt more like we were dancing than anything.Well, he was dancing; I was merely following his lead.*
*But if that was his plan—whatever his plan was—I had no choice but to dance to his beat.*
*The only thing improvisation would get me is a sword through the heart.*
*Meanwhile, sticking to the plan, I had nothing to worry about. No reason to fear his sword might go off course.*
*After several more fierce clashes, we got in close again. We were both breathing pretty heavily by that point.*
*I was drenched in sweat, and it felt like I’d collapse right then and there if I loosened up for even a second.*
*Gratien tossed a glance off to the side for the briefest of moments. His lips came together, curling up into a smile.*
Gratien: "Listen up. Three paces back, then charge. Swing down as hard as ya can! Put everything ya’ve got into it!"
Jacopo: "............"
*As soon as I signaled my acknowledgment, Gratien parried my sword off to the side.*
*One, two, three paces back, then I gathered my strength into my legs.*
*I crouched—like he had at the beginning of the duel—envisioning myself as a tiger ready to pounce.*
*And then, we leapt at each other in perfect sync.*
Jacopo: "Wha—?!"
*The harsh sound of metal striking metal—more piercing than anything else up to that point.*
*For a heartbeat, the world went white.*
*Then, the whoosh of something ripping through the air.*
Barnier: "—!"
*It wasn’t for another second or two that I realized what had happened.*
*Breathing heavily, I looked down at the sword in my hands, only to find half of it missing.*
*Gratien’s blade appeared to have snapped as well.*
*The gallery began buzzing. “Are you all right?” “How dreadfully dangerous.”*
Barnier: "............"
Gratien: "Heh..."
*A perverse grin spread across Gratien’s lips. I finally understood what he had been scheming.*
*He wanted the swords to break, and he wanted it to look like an accident, hoping that when the shattered blades went flying, a noble or two would be caught in the crossfire.*
*That explained the whole song and dance—why he had us hitting each other’s blades in the same spots again and again.*
Jacopo: "(But how the hell would you ever expect that to work?!)"
Jacopo: "Haah... haah... haaaah...! Hhhhhhhhnh!"
Jacopo: "(That said... if the fight had gone on much longer, I don’t think I would have lasted...)"
Jacopo: "............"
Jacopo: "(His plan seems to have gone about as well as you could reasonably expect. One piece landed near Barnier, and the other embedded in a table.)"
Jacopo: "(That’s fine by me. I’d rather avoid causing any more of a scene before the actual revolt.)"
Jacopo: "(...Not that I would be opposed to seeing any of these madmen skewered.)"
*Gratien wiped the sweat from his brow with the back of his hand and surveyed the room.*
*As obviously outclassed as he was by all the nobles,*
*he still seemed much larger than anyone else in the room.*
*He took a few moments to catch his breath, then sauntered over to the center of the great hall, taking another good look around the room before saying,*
Gratien: "Apologies for the... disturbance, m’lord. While ordinarily a duel’s s’posed t’ end with someone dead, it looks like that’s outta the question today."
Gratien: "So mayhap we can reschedule—put on another show in a day or two, if you would like?"
Barnier: "Hmph."
*Barnier seemed less than pleased with the outcome, but considering how rattled his guests were, he couldn’t press the matter either.*
Barnier: "Very well. Now, I believe you two deserve a reward for such a fine show."
Gratien: "Ooh! What kinda reward?"
Barnier: "One I’m sure you’ll be quite pleased with: the leftovers from today’s feast. Gourmet cuisine the likes of which you mere slaves could only dream of getting your dirty hands on."
Gratien: "Y’mean... those bleedin’ scraps o’ mystery meat?"
Barnier: "Hah. Worry not—there’s pork in there. Or does your religion prohibit you from eating pig?"
Gratien: "Nah, I gave up on religion long ago. I’m fine with eatin’ meat. ...Most meat, anyway."
Barnier: "I’ll have the other slaves bring it down this evening, then.  Now, the show’s over. Begone with you."
Gratien: "Gone we be, m’lord."
Jacopo: "(I’m still concerned about that girl... but there’s nothing I can do for her right now...)"
Jacopo: "(Hopefully she lasts long enough for the revolt, but with wounds like that...)"
Gratien: "Hey, kid, pick your beat ass up and let’s get outta here."
Jacopo: "I am not beat... thank you very much!"
Gratien: "Who’re ya tryin’ t’ fool when yer wheezin’ up a lung? Gettin’ huffy about it only makes ya look more pathetic."
Jacopo: "Piss off, you inhuman monster!"
Gratien: "Hahaha..."
Gratien: "Now, do have a wonderful day, ladies and gents."
*Once we were out of the hall and in the corridor, I took several deep breaths, finally letting loose some of the tension that had built up within me.*
*As we made our way through the corridors, the sound of the nobles laughing and chattering away amongst themselves faded into nothing.*
*We headed for the rather barren “waiting” room where slaves without assignments were kept.*
*In essence, it was a human closet.*
*I looked up at Gratien, who was standing beside me. I had about a dozen things I wanted to say to him, but I couldn’t organize my thoughts into words.*
*So I simply shrugged and pursed my lips.*
Jacopo: "I, um... yeah... That was a shame..."
Gratien: "Huh? What was a shame?"
Jacopo: "You were hoping to kill at least one noble when the swords broke, weren’t you?"
Gratien: "Hahaha! If only! I’m not that skilled, hell no."
Jacopo: "You could have fooled me..."
Gratien: "If anything, I’d say we’re lucky it went as well as it did. It’s a damned good thing they gave us their old, crusty swords, or we’d never’ve managed to break ’em at all."
Jacopo: "...If not to skewer one of the nobles, then why go to such lengths at all?"
Gratien: "Well, ’cause otherwise we’da had to fight to the death, o’ course."
Jacopo: "No, that’s not what I’m—"
Gratien: "’Course, we both know who’d be standin’ here then."
Jacopo: "............"

*Once I had Gratien on board, it was remarkable how quick the rest of the slaves were to join in.*
*They knew him, and they respected him, and that respect went a long way toward improving my own standing.*
*As far as they were concerned, I had fought Gratien and managed to hold my own.*
*And the big man was right. It wasn’t a clever plan or a moving speech these people needed to put the light back in their eyes—it was a show of strength.*
*Over the next few days, as Barnier and his guests indulged themselves, word of the plan quietly spread beneath the surface.*
*I started to see signs of life for the first time since I had arrived.*
*The majority of the slaves were emaciated and weak— there was a good possibility the guards would kill many of them.*
*But still, I urged on any who hesitated by saying,*
*“If you choose to stay, you’re guaranteed to be killed eventually.”*
*It was true, to be sure, but in essence, I was threatening them: help or be left behind to die.*
*And that was enough to convince them to grab onto the thread of hope I was offering—however frail it may be.*
*We chose the thirteenth day of the banquet to commence the revolt.*
*Waiting until almost the end of the party’s usual two-week duration gave us a slight tactical advantage.*
*Naturally, Barnier’s guests did not attend without their own protection.*
*But after being made to watch over almost two weeks of debauchery, the guards would start getting exhausted. They would stop caring.  *And that was exactly what we needed.**

*Once it started getting dark out, the kitchen became a deserted wasteland. The girls went off to work, and he went home.*
*The brothel was divided into two distinct work and living areas.*
*If it weren’t, and they used the whole building for business, I probably would have run off long ago.*
*I had only ever been in the work area during the day,*
*and though it was largely masked by the scent of various incenses burned through the night, it was filled with the unmistakable, raw stench of man.*

*In about a week’s time, Ceren had made herself thoroughly at home at the brothel.*
*She had evidently found herself quite a few fans among the clientèle, some of whom came specifically to see her.*
*Most found her peculiar, hyper-energetic disposition overwhelming at first, but quickly warmed up to her.*
*Not a single person seemed to dislike her, at least among those I came in contact with.*
*Her second week, Ceren started branching out beyond the brothel.*
*On her days off, she would go down to the pub and sing for the audience on a makeshift stage of tables.*
*Her first day performing, she dragged me with her, claiming she was too nervous to go alone. So I sat in the corner as she sang.*
*Everyone was quite surprised at how well she could sing, and they were particularly drawn to her unfamiliar melodies.*
*Perhaps they were songs from her home country, or perhaps she had picked them up elsewhere,*
*but regardless, her music and exotic appearance made for an enchanting performance.*
*When it was over, the audience roared with applause,*
*and I was convinced she was a much better choice to sing for them than me.*
*By her third week, even Maria had begun to come around.*
*At first, the most approval Ceren could squeeze out of Maria was an exasperated sigh at something dumb she said or something careless she did,*
*but as her popularity grew—and with it, the customer base—Maria softened up.*
Maria: "Man am I glad to have you working here!"
*She said with a big grin, patting Ceren on the back.*
*To me, Maria’s motivations seemed rather shallow, but considering she ran the brothel in all but name, it should have come at little surprise that she was so easily swayed by money.*
*Four weeks after her arrival, there was no one around who didn’t know Ceren.*
*The peacekeepers had all taken quite a liking to her as well—*
*particularly the number one man... The big one whose name I had trouble remembering. Whenever he ran into her, he would say,*
Gratien: "Hey there, Ceren! I’m fresh outta coin, but ya wanna spend the night together?"
Ceren: "No money, no business!"
Gratien: "Bah! Don’t be such a killjoy!"
*All the men in my life, in fact, had warmed up quite noticeably to her.*
*Including him...*
*At meals, there was seldom a quiet moment.*
*And there was nothing wrong with that.*
*I should have been pleased to see everyone so merry.*
*I should have been happy.*
*I should have celebrated their joy.*
*But by the time a full month had passed, I found myself avoiding Ceren at every opportunity.*

*I hoped her mood would improve by the time the festival came around,*
*but unsurprisingly, my hopes weren’t enough to change anything, and Morgana remained distant.*
*She had always been a sullen, aloof girl, but when she was really upset, she completely closed herself off.*
*Day after awkward day crawled by, and I soon began to wonder if inviting her had been a mistake.*
*But after all that, I couldn’t just march up to her and say “never mind, we’re not going”—*
*so I was left to endure a long, uncomfortable week, until the day the Midsummer festival commenced.*
*Midsummer, the celebration of the nativity of John the Baptist, was an event observed across the land on or around the summer solstice.*
*In our city, festivities lasted for roughly ten days.*
*A large bonfire burned in the square, vendors set up stalls selling food and trinkets,*
*and girls wore crowns made of flowers, dancing in a parade down the streets while onlookers whistled and clapped.*
*The sheer vivacity that permeated the city was enough to make one forget about the lord’s venom coursing through it.*
*Or perhaps that was the goal. An excuse to let loose and slip free of the lord’s iron grip, if only momentarily.*
*We walked down the city’s main street, observing the excitement unfolding around us.*
*Our group was only four large— the other girls either split off into their own parties...*
*or decided they would rather take the time to sleep.*
*It had been some time since I had last left the slums and visited the city proper.*
*We weren’t prohibited from entering the city, but wandering around in grimy rags only invited dirty looks, so most tended to avoid it.*
*Nobody was eager to charge headfirst into a den of prejudice.*
*But that all went away when festival time came around. Everyone was too busy enjoying themselves to go around condemning us for our appearance.*

*And a month earlier,*
*she had come to me and said I was that child.*
Jacopo: "Says the woman I’d never seen until a month ago."
Ginevra: "Physical distance has no bearing on a mother’s love."
Jacopo: "............"
Ginevra: "If you had gone to speak with the lord and revealed your lineage when I first came to see you, you would never have been in that kind of danger."
Jacopo: "............"
*Ginevra spoke as though she knew exactly what had been going through my mind this past week.*
*The very words that had dominated my thoughts as I looked out upon the wreckage.*
*If only I had done as she said and asserted my nobility,*
*then I would have had the power to prevent the attack on the brothel. To protect the people I loved.*
*If what Ginevra said was true, the Barnier family once possessed more land than even the king.*
*That land—and the family—was divided up between each of their sons.*
*The Barniers were only a few generations removed from the current royal family, which was why they controlled so much land in the first place.*
*And that meant, though it had diluted with each successive generation, there was still royal blood in the Barnier bloodline,*
*and a child begotten by the previous lord was more than entitled to a place among the nobility.*
*However, realistically speaking,*
*even if I had lent my ear to her cajolery, it wouldn’t have changed anything. Or worse, I could very well no longer be in this world.*
*I wasn’t stupid—*
*this noblewoman, she was just using me.*
Ginevra: "There is still time. Go to the lord’s manor, show him—"
Jacopo: "I’m not really your son, am I?"
*Ginevra fell silent for a moment, observing me.*
*I had no memories of my mother. Sure, it might be nice if she were to show back up someday, but it just wasn’t likely.*
*Her whole story was extremely contrived.*
*The sun could decide to rise from the west one day,*
*and there would still not be a drop of noble blood in my veins.*
*I knew perfectly well how filthy the blood in me was.*
Jacopo: "Discussions will progress much smoother if we both know what the other wants out of this arrangement. Start by telling me who you really are. Were you actually a maidservant for the previous lord?"
Ginevra: "...Yes, I was."
*She gave a nod, then walked over to a grimy shelf and retrieved a ring that looked very much out of place.*
*It was made of gold, engraved with an elaborate insignia.*
*There wasn’t a metalworker anywhere in the slums—or the city proper, at that—who could craft a piece so intricate.*
Ginevra: "Look at this. It’s the Barnier family crest. The previous lord gave me the ring as a gift,"
Ginevra: "and I’ve kept it ever since, even after our relationship was discovered and I was chased out."
Ginevra: "I didn’t sell it... because I knew I was with child."

Ginevra: "I was afraid they might kill you if I brought you in as a baby."
Jacopo: "Why were we separated?"

Ginevra: "Presumably they were hired by the Barnier estate because they didn’t want your existence coming to light."
Jacopo: "............"
Ginevra: "I consider it a miracle you’re still alive."
*I can only imagine the stern look on my face as I listened to Ginevra speak.*
*Part of me did, indeed, want to take everything she said as true.*
*Wanted to believe I was someone important. Wanted to grab hold of the fantasy that I did have noble blood in me and never let go of it.*
*And had I not come face to face with my own powerlessness a week earlier,*
*perhaps I could have swallowed the delusion—*
*used it as a steppingstone for my dream to show Morgana the world.*
Jacopo: "It would appear you’ve done your research."
Jacopo: "I don’t have any other family, to the best of my knowledge, which does indeed make me a good candidate for you to step in and claim your stake on."
Jacopo: "If I were to guess, your child with the lord has either passed away—"

Ginevra: "............"
Jacopo: "It’s probably true that you worked at the Barniers’ manor, and it’s probably true that you had relations with the former lord."
Jacopo: "You were chased out, and now you live in poverty."
Jacopo: "You want to go back to your former, more comfortable life, and while you’re at it, take vengeance on the current lord."
Jacopo: "Or perhaps you simply want the entire noble class to suffer. The specifics of your motives are irrelevant, though."

Jacopo: "Being a woman, you have no means to accomplish that yourself. So you came up with the idea of using a fake son."
Jacopo: "If your child were still alive, he would be right about my age, wouldn’t he?"
Jacopo: "And we look similar enough for you to convincingly claim we’re related."

Jacopo: "How dramatic, the man who set the lord’s slaves free was secretly his family."
Jacopo: "The people would eat it right up."

Jacopo: "............"
Ginevra: "Pfft! Hahahahahahahaha!"
Ginevra: "You’re sharp for a filthy peasant. It would seem I chose wisely."
*I was right.*
*She was a manipulative, conniving shyster.*
*But her insults didn’t bother me in the slightest. Those words made so much more sense than any mad claim that I had royal blood in me. That I was some kind of hero.*
*Following suit, I twisted my lips up into a smirk.*
*I was a filthy peasant—and I was fine with that.*
*All it meant was that I had to claw my way to the top.*
Jacopo: "I’m in."
*Ginevra and I then discussed the details of her scheme, after which she gave me the Barniers’ ring.*
*I doubt anyone would have ever imagined it would end up in the grimy hands of a slum rat.*

*What had once been nothing more than a pipe dream was, against all odds, soon to be a reality.*
*Everyone wanted the lord removed from power. They were angry and frustrated, and on several occasions had even seriously considered resorting to another revolt to make it happen.*
*But no one had been able to turn those emotions into actions— because they all knew a revolt was destined to be quashed before it made any difference.*
*That there was no hope to be found down that path.*
*But an actual revolution, that was another story— especially with someone poised to take the lord’s place on their side.*
*They had a fighting chance, and they had no reason not to take it.*
*As the fire in their eyes swelled to a blaze, mine seemed to sputter out, leaving behind a withered husk.*
*I felt as though I was sitting in a theatre, watching on as the story unfolded before me.*
*No, that didn’t quite capture how I felt. I wasn’t even watching it with my own eyes—merely listening quietly as someone recounted for me their heroic deeds.*
*The fact that I was an indispensable part of this operation still hadn’t fully sunk in.*
*All I knew was that I wasn’t completely powerless anymore.*
*But while fiery passion and hope for the future filled Gratien and the rest of the gang,*
*a dark, poisonous revulsion for God and fate streamed in to fill the hole left in me by the attack on the brothel.*
*Preparations proceeded smoothly, with Ginevra providing inside information and guidance.*
*As I had expected, there were a not-insignificant number of nobles dissatisfied with the way Barnier ruled,*
*and they were constantly looking for a way to eradicate the cloud of pestilence hanging over the land.*
*However, the lord had eyes everywhere in the upper circles, and he was especially mindful of who associated with whom—and how often. Any signs of opposition were swiftly dealt with, before they had a chance to pose any threat.*
*So the nobility needed an outsider, someone the lord wasn’t on the lookout for, to step in and assert power.*
*Ginevra arranged for several guards and attendants within the lord’s manor to betray him on the day of the revolution.*
*They would open the doors for us from the inside, and we would storm the manor.*
*Structurally, the plan was similar to the slave revolt,*
*the two major differences being my role...*
*and the lord’s death being our primary goal.*
*As the day drew nearer, it was all anyone in the slums seemed to be talking about.*
*Though we did our best to encourage everyone to stay quiet, so as to not alert the lord, it was impossible to keep the excitement contained.*
*And it wasn’t long before word reached Maria’s ears. She approached me, pale as a ghost, and attempted to talk me out of it.*
Maria: "Haven’t you had enough danger for one life?"
Maria: "C’mon, forget about all this revolution stuff."
Maria: "We can build up a new brothel and just stay low for the rest of our days. Does that really sound so bad?"
Maria: "You’re the only friend I’ve got left, Jacopo."
*She was practically begging, which she almost never did.*
*While I sympathized with her, Maria’s desperation did nothing to try my resolve.*
*And before long,*
*the day was finally upon us.*
*Before we commenced our strike on the lord’s manor, I gave a little speech in the city square.*
*Not because we actually needed them to take up arms and fight with us,*
*but because we needed to make a public proclamation of our position before overthrowing the lord.*
*They needed to know we were not merely opponents of the lord, but proponents of the common man—*
*that we had a legitimate cause and a legitimate claim to power.*
*If we were to leave them in the dark, it would only sow confusion and unrest when word of the lord’s death came out.*
*They had to see us as their liberators, not a greater evil swooping in to squash a lesser one.*
*The lord had been squeezing everything he could out of them for years, sucking them dry, so I didn’t expect anyone to actually have the willpower to join us—*
*the important thing was that they knew who I was.*
*But I had underestimated them. A slow trickle of people stood to join us, and by the time we were at the city’s edge, we had accumulated dozens of additional men,*
*transforming our little uprising into something of a much greater scale.*
*The people had been waiting, day in and day out,*
*for someone to take up their banner and lead the way.*
*It didn’t matter that the one holding the banner did so under false pretenses.*

*Unlike the slave revolt three years prior, we were entirely on the offensive this time.*

*We traded strikes, going on offense and defense in roughly equal parts.*
*Barnier was quick on his feet and quicker with his blade. When I attacked, he swayed effortlessly out of its path, like a stalk of wheat in the wind.*
*And when he attacked, he was like a typhoon.*
*He had a prowess that could never be overcome with brute strength.*
*However—*
*I had not spent the last three years idle.*
*While I could hardly imitate the refined swordsmanship of a trained nobleman,*
*I had a much better grasp on how and when to most effectively put my weight into a swing, how to maintain the ideal amount of distance between us.*

*I had no intention of running.*

*The post-revolution merrymaking continued into the wee hours of the morning. After things settled down and the men returned to city,*
*an elderly man calling himself “Odilon” showed himself before me.*
*Despite his evident age, the man stood tall and spoke in a clear, confident voice.*
*Looking into his deep sapphire eyes, I felt as though I could see everything he had borne witness to in these walls engraved within them.*
*Odilon revealed himself as an ally of the revolution and as having been in contact with Ginevra.*

*After giving me just enough time to recover, Odilon quickly got to work instructing me in everything I needed to know to be a lord.*
*My first priority was to revitalize the city— cleaning up the mess my predecessor had left it in. As much as I wanted to ensure that everyone had food and water, it simply wasn’t possible to make happen overnight.*
*We would need to significantly expand our farmland, build additional granaries, repair existing irrigation canals, not to mention crack down on crime and disorder.*
*There was so much to do it was almost dizzying, but the last thing I had time to do was whine.*
*That said, none of it could happen with a snap of the fingers. It would take time and manpower—which meant we would have to use existing stores in the meantime.*
*I asked the people—particularly among the upper-class—to relinquish ownership of their stockpiles for me to redistribute among the population. That would allow everyone to make it through the transitional period, I figured.*
*Naturally, though, not everyone was happy with the arrangement.*
*But the cries of dissatisfaction were drowned out by cheers of support when I explained that it was for everyone’s sake. That we would need to band together to carry the whole city through what would be a rocky transition.*
*As I was patting myself on the back for a job well done, Odilon shook his head and said, “’Twill hold a year at most.”*

*The lord’s manor sat atop a tall hill.*
*Gazing out, the city stretched before me—*
*countless columns of smoke rising from the buildings, the people scurrying about like so many ants.*
*From up here, I had the perfect view of the bustle of the city, but I was too far away to feel the life breathing through it.*
*To think I used to be one of those tiny little specks.*
*Thousands of people lived there. They had families, jobs, friends, hobbies. But as much as I was cognizant of that fact, I no longer grasped it on anything more than a conceptual level.*
*It was incredible just how much my view of the world had changed in merely a year’s time.*
Jacopo: "............"
Jacopo: "I’m going to go out into the world, I said..."
*I thought that clawing my way to the top of the ladder would let me see it. Would let me experience it. That with power, I could have the whole world at my fingertips.*
*But reality wasn’t so simple.*
Jacopo: "This sure isn’t what I was looking for..."
Jacopo: "Hell... I feel like the chain’s just gotten tighter around my neck..."
*There’s no way to truly grasp the view from this height without climbing up and seeing it for yourself.*
*And that was exactly where I was a year ago— blind to the reality of life in the upper echelons,*
*fantasizing about a world that didn’t exist.*
*Unlike the me of the past, I did have the satisfaction of feeling like I was accomplishing big things—*
*but was that really worth cutting myself off from all my friends?*
*Did those achievements really justify a bloody revolution?*
*Or, I suppose I should ask...*
*was this work valuable enough to me to justify it all?*
*Reminiscing about days long gone wasn’t going to help anything— and, in fact, it only served to cause me pain.*
*I couldn’t return to that time. This was where my quest for power had brought me, and now I had to live out my days as lord of this land.*
*Telling myself, day after day,*
*that it was far better than being a powerless slum rat.*
Jacopo: "(God, I’ve been so busy these past few weeks...)"
Jacopo: "(My eyes are...)"
Jacopo: "One day, I’ll show you the world."

*How I first needed to improve the economy as a whole before I could turn my attention to the less well-off—*
*that “nothing changing” was proof I was protecting them—*
*that the slums had been in danger of being eradicated entirely, and I had stopped it!*
*But I could see in his eyes that nothing I said would convince him.*
*As far as he was concerned,*
*I was pure evil.*

*In a flash, everything I once felt for this man— all the friendship, the camaraderie, the sympathy— went cold.*
*As much as he tried to dress up this whole farce, that was where his heart truly was.*
*He was just sour about not being the one who got to be lord.*
*He believed himself superior.*
Jacopo: "(He got himself caught up in this stupid fit of jealousy...)"
Jacopo: "(and now he’s trying to kill me over it?)"

*I appointed a new consul shortly after Odilon’s death, but he was an ineffectual, shortsighted oaf.*
*And to top it off, he had a tendency to simply nod and agree with anyone and everyone rather than express any opinions of his own.*
*If anything, having him around made my job more difficult.*
*But it wasn’t as though there was anyone else competent enough to take the position, and relieving him so soon would come with its own crop of problems.*
*So I ended up using him to shuffle papers and handle other menial work while I took care of the actual decision-making.*
*My first order of business was to establish clearly defined rules and punishments.*
*Speaking ill of the lord, spreading false rumors about his lineage, creating art of any form which could be interpreted as defamatory—*
*these were all considered acts of lèse-majesté and, in the most severe cases, were potentially punishable by death.*
*I started seeing results almost immediately.*
*The people stopped claiming I was a fake, that I came from the slums. The Barnier name became a symbol of fear once again.*
*My renovation efforts proceeded as originally planned. The city flourished as a commerce hub, attracting merchants and patrons and visitors from near and far.*
*Some fields had to be removed as well to accommodate the expansion of the marketplace, but they were hardly producing anything worthwhile to begin with.*
*If farmers wanted to keep farming, they were free to move elsewhere.*
*I scaled down the size of the slums while scaling up security.*
*Lacking leadership, what little remained of the peacekeeper group had turned into a gang of unruly thugs,*
*so rather than keeping the peace they largely made things worse.*
*In response, I forbade the formation of unauthorized organizations within the slums.*
*And with all that in place, the city was much safer and more orderly, and the economy significantly stronger than it had been under my predecessor’s rule.*
*However, those unfortunate enough to miss riding the wave of economic growth found themselves falling ever further behind their neighbors.*
*The ravine between the poor and the wealthy had become nigh uncrossable.*
*My own manor was hardly a bastion of safety either.*
*Not only was I rapidly enacting new policy, laws were stricter and punishments harsher than before, so many were—to put it lightly—more than a little displeased with me.*
*I’d gone through several food tasters, and just as many direct assassination attempts.*
*It wasn’t particularly difficult to avoid major incidents by simply suspecting everyone, but I could seldom relax as a result.*
*In time I came to prefer solitude.*
*Well, not so much prefer...*
*as require it for my own safety.*
*Odilon’s death left me with more than just solitude, though—*
*the phantom slipped free from my dreams...*
*appearing from my shadow whenever I was alone...*
*laughing endlessly.*
Barnier: "What did I tell you, boy? You’re controlling them with fear, just as I did."
Jacopo: "............"

*I started relying on alcohol to rid myself of the phantom.*
*When I was drunk enough, I didn’t have to see anything. I didn’t dream, I didn’t have nightmares.*
*And most importantly, I didn’t have to think.*
*There were times I couldn’t be sure whether I was in my right mind.*
*There were times I was so panicked I wanted to scream my throat raw.*
*But there was never a time I was safe showing that weakness. I knew from experience it would only put me at risk.*
*I couldn’t complain to anyone.*
*I couldn’t ask anyone for help.*
*I couldn’t believe in anyone.*
*Odilon’s dying words came back to me again and again. “Stay strong, and ever high of heart.”*
*What did it even mean to be high of heart, though?*
*Certainly I had nothing to be proud of—*
*not having spent my early years a filthy, impoverished slum rat; not having spearheaded a violent revolution and stolen the throne; not this farce of a tale about me secretly being a noble.*
*I hadn’t been raised to be a lord. I hadn’t been taught to have a proud, noble spirit.*
*That was likely what Odilon had wanted to impart upon me—*
*the nature of a ruler.*
*People automatically respect a man who is truly dignified and noble. They instinctively admire him, kneeling out of wholehearted honor.*
*And that’s what makes a true king.*
*Not knowing the first thing about how to be that kind of man, I fell back on the only alternative that came to mind: forcing them to kneel beneath my iron fist.*
*Two years under Odilon’s tutelage was nowhere near long enough to instill in me the natural nobility I needed.*
*Thus was I left to be tormented by this accursed phantom whenever alone and play the ruthless ruler elsewise.*
*In my attempts to mask the fear lurking within, I grew louder and more ostentatiously contemptuous.*
*Practically every other word out of my mouth was an insult.*
*The old Jacopo was long gone, and “Lord Barnier” had crept in to take his place.*
*It was gradual enough that I hardly realized it was happening. But now, all I saw in the mirror was the face of a cruel, wretched man.*
*I even came to hate my old self. Think him foolish.*
*What kind of a vapid, nebulous dream was “I want to go out into the world”? What basis did I have for thinking myself any more significant than any other kid on the streets?*
*And where the hell did I get the idea that having influence meant having freedom?*
*I could barely even take care of myself—where did I get off on thinking a little power would let me protect the people I cared about?*
*And what about now?*
*What, fundamentally, did I even want now? What did I have to protect?*
*Nothing. I was too far gone.*
*There was no light to be found no matter how far I marched this path,*
*and only a bottomless abyss waited at my back...*
*I had left myself... with nowhere to return to.*
*Before I knew it, nearly four years had passed since the revolution.*
*By then, not a soul dared defy my will. I was surrounded by people who supported my every decision.*
*Few of them genuinely agreed with me on everything, obviously,*
*but no one could deny how much growth I had brought about, and so long as I continued to produce results, I would continue to have their outward support.*
*As a trading hub, the city flourished, but the citizens were less than thrilled about the work I was doing. The poorest, especially, seemed to hold a great deal of enmity for me.*
*But I was reaching the point where I couldn’t manage on the support of the upper crust alone.*
*Simply dumping sacks of coin into the streets wouldn’t end poverty, though, and increasing taxes on imported goods would undermine everything I had built up.*
*Lacking an actual solution, all I could do was work to make them feel like they were better off than they actually were.*
*Fortunately, it was much easier to pull the wool over the middle- and lower-class’s eyes than it was the better educated upper-class.*
*I started by building public recreational facilities, which citizens had priority access to,*
*and then I turned my eyes toward religion.*
*About a year earlier, as a part of the city’s expansion, a bridge was built across the river that served as the city’s effective border.*
*A church had still not been established in that new district, so I wanted to build one and gather a following.*
*Conveniently enough, my predecessor had been in the process of building a second manor on the other side of the river, and with a little bit of work, it could easily be repurposed into a church.*
*Once people started making use of the church, it would be simple to win their favor by making myself known as a generous patron.*
*Faith was the ultimate blinder for the uneducated, and history showed time and again that religion could be a powerful tool in the right hands.*
*But the high foreigner and merchant population in the city meant that no one particular religion had managed to take hold.*
*The merchants especially tended to develop their own individual philosophies over the years, with few caring much for higher powers.*
*That was a possible explanation for why the Church had never had much sway in this region.*
*There was, therefore, a chance the whole thing would just be a colossal waste of money.*
*However, I had recently caught wind of a curious rumor:*
*there was a nun serving at a small church at the far edge of town who people had come to refer to as “the Saintess.”*

*In addition to the renovations for the church and gathering intel on the Far Eastern man, I made sure to keep an eye out for any unusual rumors or happenings outside the city’s borders.*
*Of all the reports I received, the one that caught my attention was word of a witch who lived by a lake.*
*This lakeside witch supposedly had miraculous powers, allowing her to cure any ailment—*
*and the effect was transmitted through her blood.*
*This wasn’t the first time I had heard of the witch— an elderly woman who prepared and sold medicinal herbs— but I hadn’t realized she also traded in miracles.*
*It sounded like a bunch of snake oil to me, but she had allegedly cured a deathly ill young girl and started to make quite a name for herself.*

*If I had known she was a young girl—let alone her!—*
*I would have never given the command!*

*I couldn’t change it.*

*It didn’t matter how we had gotten here,*
*or what I had felt about her,*

*Once, I had been sold off as a saint, a highly valuable commodity... but this time around, I was cheap, expendable labor.*

*I stood aghast as the massacre unfolded before me. Blood pooled at my feet. Severed limbs were strewn about the carriage.*

*Men, women, children. Everyone equals at the end of his blade.*

*I had witnessed so much death in so short a time.*
